♦Audits (Verification); Colleges; *Compliance (Legal); 
♦Federal Aid; Higher Education; ♦Student Financial Aid 
♦Office of Management and Budget 



ABSTRACT 



This document is a supplement to Circular A- 133 (1990) from 

the Office of Management and Budget (0MB), which extended the government's 
"single audit process" for agencies that administer federal financial 
assistance programs to higher education institutions and non-profit 
organizations. This supplement is based on the 1996 Amendments (the Single 
Audit Act Amendments of 1996 based on the Single Audit Act of 1984 which 
established requirements for audits of states, local governments, and Indian 
tribal governments that administer federal financial assistance programs) and 
the final revision of 0MB Circular A- 133, "Audits of Institutions of Higher 
Education and Other Non-Profit Organizations, " which provide for the issuance 
of a compliance statement to assist auditors in performing the required 
audits. It identifies compliance requirements that the Federal government 
expects to be considered as part of an audit under the 1996 Amendments. It 
enables auditors to avoid time-consuming research into the requirements that 
must be met. The Supplement provides auditors with the federal program's 
objectives, procedures, and compliance requirements relevant to the audit as 
well as audit objectives and suggested audit procedures for determining 
compliance with these requirements. For single audits, the supplement 
replaces agency audit guides and other audit requirement documents for 
individual federal programs. The guide is divided into these sections: (1) 

"Background, Purpose, and Applicability"; (2) "Matrix of Compliance 
Requirements"; (3) "Compliance Requirements"; (4) "Agency Program 
Requirements"; (5) "Clusters of Programs"; (6) "Internal Control"; and (7) 
"Guidance for Auditing Programs Not Included in this Compliance Supplement." 
Part 4 lists specific requirements for many financial assistance programs of 
the U.S. Department of Education. Including Title I and TRIO programs, and 
part 5 contains guidance for Department of Education clusters of programs, 
including federal student loan programs such as the Perkins, Pell, and Direct 
Loan programs. Eight appendixes provide additional details, background 
information, and some internal reference materials. (SLD) 



Reproductions supplied by EDRS are the best that can be made 
from the original document.

Background, Purpose, and Applicability 



PART 1 - BACKGROUND, PURPOSE, AND APPLICABILITY 

BACKGROUND 

The Single Audit Act of 1984 established requirements for audits of States, local governments, 
and Indian tribal governments that administer Federal financial assistance programs. In 1985, 
the Office of Management and Budget (0MB) issued 0MB Circular A-128, "Audits of State and 
Local Governments," to provide implementing guidance. In 1990, 0MB administratively 
extended the single audit process to non-profit organizations by issuing 0MB Circular A- 133, 
"Audits of Institutions of Higher Education and Other Non-Profit Organizations." 

On July 5, 1996, the President signed the Single Audit Act Amendments of 1996 (3 1 USC 
Chapter 75). The 1996 Amendments extended the statutoiy audit requirement to non-ppfit 
organizations and substantially revised various provisions of the 1984 Act. On June 30, 1997, 
0MB issued final revisions to Circular A-133 (62 FR 35278). The final revisions implement the 
1996 Amendments, extend 0MB Circular A- 133's coverage to States, local goveniments, and 
Indian tribal governments, and rescind 0MB Circular A-128. 

This Compliance Supplement is based on the requirements of the 1996 Amendments and the 
final revision of 0MB Circular A-133 which provide for the issuance of a compliance 
supplement to assist auditors in performing the required audits. The Senate and House Reports 
supporting the 1996 Amendments cited studies of the single audit process performed by the 
General Accounting Office, the President's Council oo Integrity and Efficiency and the National 
State Auditors Association (NSAA). All three studies supported the need for a cument 
compliance supplement. The NSAA study stated, "The Compliance Supplement provides an 
invaluable tool to both Federal agencies and auditors in setting forth the important provisions of 
Federal assistance progi’ams. This tool allows Federal agencies to effectively communicate items 
which they believe ai’e important to the successful management of the program and legislative 
intent . . . Such a valuable tool requires constant review and update." 

This document serves to identify existing important compliance requirements which the Federal 
Government expects to be considered as part of an audit required by the 1 996 Amendments. 
Without this Supplement, auditors would need to reseai’ch many laws and regulations for each 
progi’am under audit to detennine which compliance requirements are important to the Federal 
Government and could have a direct and material effect on a program. Providing this 
Supplement is a more efficient and cost effective approach to performing this research. For the 
progi-ams contained herein, this Supplement provides a source of infoimation for auditors to 
understand the Federal program's objectives, procedures, and compliance requirements relevant 
to the audit as well as audit objectives and suggested audit procedures for deteimining 
compliance with these requirements. 

This Supplement also provides guidance to assist auditors in determining compliance 
requirements relevant to the audit, audit objectives, and suggested audit procedures for programs 
not included herein. For single audits, this Supplement replaces agency audit guides and other 
audit requirement documents for individual Federal programs. 



O 



■\-133 Compliance Supplement 



1-1 



0MB Circular A-1 33 provides that Federal agencies are responsible to annually inform 0MB of 
any updates needed to this Supplement. This responsibility includes ensuring that program 
objectives, procedures, and compliance requirements, noncompliance with which could have a 
direct and material effect on these individual Federal programs, are provided to 0MB for 
inclusion in this Supplement, and that agencies keep cuiTent these program objectives, 
pioceduies, and compliance requirements i.^mciuding statutoi^y and regulatoiy citations). To 
facilitate agency efforts to meet this responsibility, Paits 4 and 5 of this Supplement provide a 
Stand-alone section for each program included in this Supplement which contains program 
objectives, piogiam procedures, and compliance requirements. For some programs a separate 
subsection (IV. Other Infoiraation), is also included to communicate additional information 
concerning the program. For example, when a program allows funds to be transfeired to another 
piogram, subsection IV will provide guidance on how those funds should be treated on the 
Schedule of Expendituies of Federal Awards and Type A progi'am determinations. See 
Appendix FV for a list of programs which contain this subsection. These individual sections can 
be updated or replaced as Federal programs change. Also, sections will be added for additional 
programs once the program objectives, program procedures, and compliance requirements 
relevant to the program are written.

Background, Purpose, and Applicability 



PURPOSE AND APPLICABILITY (Part 1) 

Purpose 

This Supplement is effective for audits of fiscal years beginning after June 30, 1999, and 
supei-sedes the 0MB Circular A-133 Compliance Supplement issued in April 1999. 

0MB Circular A-133 describes the non-Federal entity's responsibilities for managing Fedei-al 

assistance programs (§ .300) and the auditor's responsibility with respect to the scope of audit 

.500). Auditors are required to follow the provisions of 0MB Circular A-133 and this 
Supplement. 

Applicability 

General 

Auditors shall consider this Supplement and the referenced laws, regulations, and 0MB Circulars 
(as codified by Federal agencies in agency regulations) in detennining the compliance 
requirements that could have a direct and material effect on the programs included herein. That 
is, use of this Supplement is mandatory. Accordingly, adherence to this Supplement satisfies the 
requirements of 0MB Circular A-133. For progi'am-specific audits perfonnedin accordance 
with a Federal agency’s progi'am-specific audit guide, the auditor shall follow such program- 
specific audit guide. Finally, for major programs not included in this Supplement, the auditor 
shall follow the guidance in Pail 7 and use the types o^ compliance requirements in Pail 3 to 
identify the applicable compliance requirements which could have a direct and material effect on 
the progi'am. 

Update of Requirements 

0MB Circular A-133 provides that Federal agencies are responsible to annually inform 0MB of 
any updates needed to this Supplement. However, auditors should recognize that laws and 
regulations change periodically and that delays will occur between such changes and revisions to 
this Supplement. Moreover, auditors should recognize that there may be provisions of contract 
and grant agreements that ai'e not specified in law or regulation and, therefore, the specifics of 
such are not included in this Supplement. For example, the grant agreement may specify a 
certain matching percentage or set a priority for how funds should be spent (e.g., a requirement to 
not fund certain size projects). Another example is a Federal agency imposing additional 
requirements on a recipient because it is high-risk in accordance with the A- 102 Common Rule, 
or as pait of resolution of prior audit findings. 

Accordingly, the auditor should perfoim reasonable procedures to ensure that compliance 
requirements are cuirent and to deteimine whether there are any additional provisions of contract 
and grant agreements that should be covered by an audit under the 1996 Amendments. 
Reasonable procedures would be inquiry of non-Federal entity management and review of the 
contract and grant agreements for programs selected for testing (i.e., major programs). 



ERJC 



'”133 Compliance Supplement 



1-3 



Safe Harbor Status 

Because the suggested audit procedures were written to be able to apply to many different 
programs administered by many different entities, they ai'e necessaiily general in nature. Auditor 
judgment will be necessaiy to determine whether the suggested audit procedures ai'e sufficient to 
achieve the stated audit objectives or whether additional or alternative audit procedures are 
needed. Therefore, the auditor should not consider this Supplement to be a "safe harbor" for 
identifying the audit procedures to apply in a particular engagement. 

However, the auditor can consider this Supplement a "safe haibor" for identification of 
compliance requirements to be tested for the programs included herein if, as discussed above, the 
auditor (1) performs reasonable procedures to ensure that the requirements in this Supplement are 
cuirent and to determine whether there are any additional provisions of contract and grant 
agreements that should be covered by an audit under the 1996 Amendments, and (2) upHates or 
augments the requirements contained in this Supplement as appropriate. 

Responsibility for Other Requirements 

Although the focus of this Supplement is on compliance requirements that could have a direct 
and material effect on a major program, auditors also have responsibility under Generally 
Accepted Government Auditing Standards (GAGAS) for other requirements when specific 
infonnation comes to the auditor' attention that provides evidence concerning the existence of 
possible noncompliance that could have a material indirect effect on a major program.

Background, Purpose, and Applicability 



OVERVIEW OF TfflS SUPPLEMENT 
Matrix of Compliance Requirements (Part 2) 

The Matnx of Compliance Requirements (Matrix) identifies the Federal programs and 
compliance requirements addressed by this Supplement and associates the programs with the 
applicable compliance requirements. The Matiix also identifies the applicable Federal agency 
and Catalog of Federal Domestic Assistance (CFDA) number for each program included in this 
Supplement. 

Compliance Requirements (Part 3) 

Part 3 lists and describes the 14 types of compliance requirements and, except for Special Tests 
and Provisions, the related audit objectives that the auditor shall consider in every audit 
conducted under 0MB Circulai' A-133, with the exception of program-specific audits perfonned 
in accordance with a Federal agency's program-specific audit guide. Suggested audit procedui’es 
are also provided to assist the auditor in planning and perfoiming tests of non-Federal entity 
compliance with the requirements of Federal programs. Auditor judgment will be necessaiy to 
deteimine whether the suggested audit procedures ai'e sufficient to achieve the stated audit 
objectives and whether additional or alternative audit procedures are needed. Determining the 
nature, timing, and extent of the audit procedures necessaiy to meet the audit objectives is the 
auditor's responsibility. 

The compliance requirements for Special Tests and Provisions are unique to each Federal 
program; therefore, compliance requirements, audit objectives, and suggested audit procedures 
for Special Tests and Provisions ai'e not included in Part 3. 

Because of the diversity of systems in place among non-Federal entities. Part 3 does not include 
suggested audit procedures to test internal conti'ol. The auditor must determine appropriate 
procedures to test internal control on a case by case basis considering factors such as the non- 
Federal entity's internal conUol, the compliance requirements, the audit objectives for 
compliance, the auditor's assessment of conti’ol risk, and the audit requirement to test internal 
conti'ol as prescribed in 0MB Circular A-133. 

Agency Program Requirements (Part 4) 

For each Federal program included. Part 4 discusses program objectives, program procedures, 
and compliance requirements which ai'e specific to the program. With the exception of Special 
Tests and Provisions, the auditor shall refer to Part 3 for the audit objectives and suggested audit 
procedures that pertain to the compliance requirements associated with the programs. Since 
Special Tests and Provisions ai'e unique to the program, the audit objectives and suggested audit 
procedures for the progi'am ai'e included in Part 4. 

The description of progi'am procedures is general in nature. Some programs may operate 
somewhat differently than described due to: (1) the complexity of governing Federal and State 
laws and regulations; (2) the administrative flexibility afforded non-Federal entities; and, (3) the 



O ■ 



133 Compliance Supplement 



1-5 



IVXiliCU 






nature, size, and volume of transactions involved. Accordingly, the auditor should obtain an 
understanding of the applicable compliance requirements and program procedures in operation at 
the non-Federal entity to properly plan and perform the audit. 



Clusters of Programs (Part 5) 



A cluster of progi’ams is a gi’ouping of closely related programs that have similai' compliance 
requirements. The types of clusters are: Research and Development (R&amp;D), Student Financial 
Aid (SFA), and other clusters. "Other clusters" are as identified in this Supplement or designated 
in a State awai'd document. 



Although the programs within a cluster are administered as sepai'ate progi’ams, a cluster of 
programs is ti’eated as a single progi am for the puipose of meeting the audit requirements of 

0MB Circular A-133 (§ .105). Part 5 provides compliance requirements, audit objectives, and 

suggested audit procedures for R&amp;D and SFA clusters and lists other clusters. 

In planning and perfoiming the audit, the auditor should determine whether programs 
administered by the non-Federal entity ai’e part of a cluster by refemng to the provisions of Pait 
5 of this Supplement and the State award documents. 

Internal Control (Part 6) 

As a condition of receiving Federal awards, non-Fedpral entities agree to comply with applicable 
laws, regulations, and the provisions of conti’act and grant agreements, and to maintain internal 
control to provide reasonable assurance of compliance with these requirements. 0MB Circular 
A-133 requires auditors to obtain an understanding of the non-Federal entity's internal conti’ol 
over Federal programs sufficient to plan the audit to support a low assessed level of control risk 
for major programs, plan the testing of internal control over major programs to support a low 
assessed level of control risk for the assertions relevant to the compliance requirements for each 
major progi’am, and, unless internal control is likely to be ineffective, perfoiTn testing of internal 
control as planned. Part 6 is intended to assist non-Federal entities and their auditors in 
complying with these requirements by presenting characteristics of internal control which may be 
used to reasonably ensure compliance with the types of compliance requirements in Part 3. The 
characteristics of internal control presented in Part 6 ai’e neither mandatoiy nor all inclusive. 

Guidance for Auditing Programs Not Included in this Compliance Supplement (Part 7) 

Part 7 provides guidance to auditors in identifying the compliance requirements and designing 
tests of compliance with such requirements for progi’ams not included in this Supplement. 

Federal Programs Excluded from the A-102 Common Rule (Appendix I) 

This Appendix lists block grants and entitlement programs excluded from the requirements of the 
"Unifoi-m Administi’ative Requirements for Grants and Cooperative Agreements to State and 
Local Governments" (also known as the"A-102 Common Rule").

Federal Agency Codification of Certain Governmentwide Grants Requirements (Appendix 

II)

Common Rule and 0MB Circular A-1 10, "Uniform Administrative Requirements for Gi-ants and

Organizations," in agency regulations. Some agencies have not yet codified the November 1993 
revision to 0MB Circular A-1 10 but either are in the process of doing so or have provided such 
policies to grantees through other means such as grant agreements. 

Federal Agency Contacts for A-133 Audits (Appendix ID) 

This Appendix identifies Federal agency contacts from which auditors can request information or 
materials about Federal programs or the audit requirements of 0MB Circular A-1 33. 

Internal Reference Tables (Appendix IV) 

This Appendix provides a listing of programs in Parts 4 and 5 which include 'TV. Other 
Information." This listing allows the auditor to quickly determine which programs have other 
information such as guidance on Type A and Type B progi-am determination or display on the 
Schedule of Expenditures of Federal Awai’ds. Also identified in this Appendix is that the 
Medicaid Cluster is the only program cuirently identified as higher risk by 0MB pursuant to 
Circular A-133, § .525(c)(2). 

List of Changes for the 2000 Compliance Supplement (Appendix V)

Other 0MB Circular A-133 Advisories (Appendix VI)

under the Food Stamps program (CFDA 10.551) in accordance with the American Institute of 
Certified Public Accountants (AICPA) Statement on Auditing Standai'ds (SAS) No. 70, Service 
Organizations. 

Compliance Supplement Core Team (Appendix VIII) 

This Appendix provides a listing of the Compliance Supplement Core Team members who were 
responsible for the production of this Supplement.

TECHNICAL INFORMATION 



Page Numbering Scheme 

The following page numbering scheme is used in this Supplement to facilitate future revisions. 

Each page included in Parts 1, 2, 3 (introduction), 6 (inti’oduction), and 7 is identified by a label 
that repi-esents the part number and sequential page number. A dash (-) separates the part 

number fi'om the page number. For example, Part 1 is numbered as follows: 1-1, 1-2, 1-3, and so 
on. 



Each page included in Parts 3 (excluding introduction), 4, 5, and 6 (excluding introduction) is 
identified by a label that represents the part number, section number identifier, and sequential 
page number. For example. Section A of Part 3 is numbered 3-A-l, 3-A-2, 3-A-3, and s*o on. 
The section number identifier for Part 4 represents the CFDA number of the applicable program. 
For example, the Department of Labor (DOL) Unemployment Insurance program, CFDA 
nurriber 17.225, is numbered 4-17.225-1, 4-17.225-2, 4-17.225-3, and so on. 

Code of Federal Regulations 

The Code of Federal Regulations (CFR) is a codification of the rules issued by Federal agencies. 
The CFR is divided into 50 titles which comprise the broad areas subject to Federal regulation. 
Each title is further divided into parts and sections, wjth most references to the CFR being made 
at this level. 

Portions of the CFR are revised daily and these changes are published in the Federal Register. 
However, a revised version of the CFR is published only once each calendar yeai‘, on aquaiterly 
basis as follows: titles 1-16 on Januai 7 1, titles 17-27 on April 1, titles 28-41 on July 1, and titles 
42-50 on October 1 . 

In the event that changes to a particulai- section of a title have changed since the last published 
update of that section, a notation is made in the List of CFR Sections Affected (LS A), which is 
published monthly. The LSA cites the Federal Register page number which contains tiie 
changes to the CFR section. 

In order to obtain the most cuirent regulations, the user should consult not onlythe latest version 
of the CFR, but also the LSA issued in the current month. The Federal Register home page 
(http://www.access.gpo.gov/nai-a/) offera links to both the Federal Register and die CFR. Please 
note that on-line versions of the CFR may not be the most current available.

PART 2 - MATRIX OF COMPLIANCE REQUIREMENTS 

INTRODUCTION 

This Part identifies the compliance requirements that ai'e applicable to the programs included in 
this Supplement. Because Pail 4, Agency Program Requirements and Pait 5 , Clusters of 
Progi'ams do not include guidance for all types of compliance requirements that pertain to the 
program, (see introduction to Part 4 for additional infoimation), the auditor should use this Pait 
to identify the types of compliance requirements that are applicable. The boxes for each type of 
compliance requirement will either contain a "Y" (for yes if the type of compliance requirement 
may apply) or be shaded (if the program noimally does not have activity subject to this type of 
compliance requirement). 

Even though a "Y" indicates that the compliance requirement applies to the Federal progi^hra, it 
may not apply at a particular non-Federal entity, either because that entity does not have activity 
subject to that type of compliance requirement or the activity could not have a material effect on 
a major program. For example, even though Real Propeity Acquisition/Relocation Assistance 
may apply to a paiticular program, it would not apply to a non-Federal entity that did not acquire 
real propeity covered by the Uniform Relocation Assistance and Real Property Acquisition 
Policies Act. Similaily, a "Y" may be included under "Procurement," however, the audit would 
not be expected to address this type of compliance requirement if the non-Federal entity charges 
only small amounts of purchases to a major program. The auditor should exercise professional 
judgment when deteimining which compliance requirements mai-ked " Y" need to be tested at a 
paiticular non-Federal entity. 

When a "Y" is present on the matrix and the auditor determines that the requirement should be 
tested at the non-Federal entity, the auditor should use Pait 3, Compliance Requirements, and 
Parts 4 (or 5), if applicable, in planning and performing the tests of compliance. For example, if 
a program entiy in the mahix includes a "Y" in the Progi-am Income column, Pait 3 provides a 
general description of the compliance requirement. Part 3 also provides the audit objective and 
the suggested audit procedures for testing progi’am income. Part 4 (or 5) may also include 
specific infoimation on progi'am income criteria pertaining to the program, such as restrictions on 
how progi’am income may be used. Part 6, Internal Control, may be useful in assessing conhol 
risk and designing tests of internal conhol with respect to each applicable compliance 
requirement. 

When a compliance requirement is shaded in the matrix, it normally does not apply to the 
program. However, if specific infoimation comes to the auditor's attention (e.g., during the 
noimal review of the grant agreement or discussions with management) that provides evidence 
that a compliance requirement shaded in the matrix could have a material effect on a major 
progi’am, the auditor would be expected to test the requirement. This circumstance should ai’ise 
infrequently.

Compliance Requirements 



PART 3 - COMPLIANCE REQUIREMENTS 

INTRODUCTION 

The objectives of most compliance requirements for Federal programs administered by States, 
local governments, Indian tribal governments, and non-profit organizations are geneiic in nature. 
For example, most programs.have eligibility requirements for individuals or organizations. 

While the criteria for determining eligibility vaiy by program, the objective of the compliance 
requirement that only eligible individuals or organizations participate is consistent across all 
programs. 

Rather than repeat these compliance requirements, audit objectives, and suggested audit « 
procedures for each of the programs contained in Part 4 - Agency Program Requirements and 
Pait 5 -.Clusters of Programs, they are provided once in this pait. For each program in this 
Compliance Supplement (this Supplement), Part 4 or Pait 5 contains additional infoimation 
about the compliance requirements that arise fi'om laws and regulations applicable to each 
program, including the requirements specific to each program that should be tested using the 
guidance in this pait. 

Administrative Requirements 

The administrative requirements that apply to most programs arise from two sources: the 
"UnifoiTn Administrative Requirements for Gi-ants and Cooperative Agi'eements to State and 
Local Governments" (also known as the "A-,102 Common Rule") and 0MB Circular A-1 10, 
"Unifoim Administi'ative Requirements for Gi'ants and Agi'eements with Institutions of Higher 
Education, Hospitals, and Other Non-Profit Organizations," and the agencies' codification of 
0MB Circular A-1 10. The applicable guidance followed depends on the type of organization 
undergoing audit. Other administrative compliance requirements unique to a single program or a 
cluster of programs, ai‘e provided in the Special Tests and Provisions sections of Pails 4 and 5. 

State, Local, and Indian Tribal Governments 

Govemmentwide guidance for administering grants and cooperative agreements to States, local 
goveiTiments, and Indian tiibal governments is contained in the A-1 02 Common Rule which was 
codified by each Federal funding agency in its volume of the Code of Federal Regulations. The 
A-102 Common Rule section numbers are refemed to without the Federal agency’s part number 
(e.g., § .37 would refer to sections in all agency regulations). This allows auditors to refer to 

the same section numbers when discussing administi'ative issues with different Federal funding 
agencies. 

These requirements apply to all grants and subgrants to governments, except gi-ants and 
subgi'ants to State or local (public) institutions of higher education and hospitals, and except 
where they are inconsistent with Federal statutes or with regulations authorized in accordance 
with the exception provision of the A-102 Common Rule. Block grants authorized by the 
Omnibus Budget Reconciliation Act of 1981 and several other specifically identified gi'ants or 



O 



133 Compliance Supplement 



3-1 



March 2000 



Compliance Requirements 



payment programs are exempted from the A- 102 Common Rule. Appendix I to this Compliance 
Supplement lists legislation and programs where exclusions exist. 

In some cases the A-102 Common Rule permits States to follow their own laws and procedures, 
e.g., when addressing equipment management. These are noted in the sections that follow. The 
auditor will have to refer to an individual State's rules in those situations. 

Non-Profit Organizations 

The major source of requirements applicable to institutions of higher education, hospitals and 
other non-profit organizations is 0MB Circulai- A-1 10. The provisions of 0MB Circula- A-1 10 
are codified in agency regulations, generally following the section numbers in the circulai? The 
0MB Circula- A-1 10 section numbers ai'e referred to similai- to the A-102 Common Rule 
references. However, unlike the A-102 Common Rule, agencies with 0MB approval, could 
modify certain provisions of A-1 10 to meet their special needs. 0MB Circulai- A-1 10 states 
"Federal agencies responsible for awai'ding and administering grants . . . shall adopt the language 
in the circular unless different provisions are required by Federal statute or a'e approved by 

0MB." Subpart A, § .4, of 0MB Circulai- A-1 10 states that "Federal awai-ding agencies may 

apply more rest-ictive requirements to a class of recipients when approved by 0MB." Federal 
awarding agencies may apply less resti-ictive requirements when awarding small awards, except 
for those requirements which ai-e statutoiy. Exceptions on a case-by-case basis may also be 
made by Federal awarding agencies. 

Appendix II to this supplement contains a list of agencies that have codified 0MB Circular- A- 
110 and the CFR citations for these codifications. 

Subrecipients 

Governmental subrecipients axe subject to the provisions of the A-102 Common Rule. However, 
the A-102 Common Rule pemits States to impose their own requirements on their governmental 
subrecipients, e.g., equipment management or procurement. Thus, in some circumstances, the 
auditor may need to refer to State mles and regulations rather than Federal requirements. 

All subrecipients who are institutions of higher education, hospitals, or other non-profits, 
regai-dless“Of the type of organization malcingthe subawai'd, shall follow the provisions of 0MB 
Circulai- A-1 10 as implemented by the agency when awai'ding or administering subgrants except 
under block grants aufiiorized by the Omnibus Budget Reconciliation Act of 198 1 and the Job 
Training Pai-tnership Act where State mles apply instead. 

Compliance Requirements, Audit Objectives, and Suggested Audit Procedures 

Auditors shall consider the compliance requhements and related audit objectives in Part 3 and 
Part 4 or 5 (for programs included in this Supplement) in eveiy audit of non-Federal entities 
conducted under 0MB Circular A- 133, with the exception of program-specific audits perfoi-med 
in accordance with a Federal agency's progi-am-specific audit guide. In making a detemination 
not to test a compliance requirement, the auditor must conclude that the requirement either does 



A-1 33 Corr^liance Supplement 



3-2 



March 2000 



Conyliance Requirements 



not apply to the particular non-Federal entity or that noncompliance with the requirement could 
not have a material effect on a major program (e.g., the auditor would not be expected to test 
Procurement if the non-Federal entity charges only small amounts of purchases to a major 
program). The descriptions of the compliance requirements in Parts 3, 4, and 5 are generally a 
summaiy of the actual compliance requirements. The auditor should refer to the referenced 
citations (e.g., laws and regulations) for the complete compliance requirements. 

The suggested audit procedures ai‘e provided to assist auditors in planning and peifoiming tests 
of non-Federal entity compliance with the requirements of Federal progi’ams. Auditor judgment 
will be necessary to deteimine whether the suggested audit procedures are sufficient to achieve 
the stated audit objective and whether additional or alternative audit procedures are needed. 

The suggested procedures are in lieu of specifying audit procedures for each of the programs 
included in this Supplement. This approach has several advantages. First, it provides guidelines 
to assist auditors in designing audit procedures that ai’e appropriate in the circumstance. Second, 
it helps auditors develop audit procedures for programs that ai’e not included in this Supplement. 
Finally, it simplifies future updates to this Supplement. 

Internal Control 

Because of the diversity of systems in place among non-Federal entities. Part 3 does not include 
suggested audit procedures to test internal control. The auditor must deteimine appropriate 
procedures to test internal control on a case by case basis considering factors such as the non- 
Federal entity's internal control, the compliance requirements, the audit objectives for 
compliance, the auditor's assessment of coriti’ol risk, and the audit requirement to test internal 
conti’ol as prescribed in 0MB Circular A- 133.

Con^iliance Requirements 



A. ACTIVITIES ALLOWED OR UNALLOWED 

Compliance Requirements 

The specific requirements for activities allowed or unallowed are unique to each Federal program 
and are found in the laws, regulations, and the provisions of contract or grant agreements 
pertaining to the program. For programs listed in die Compliance Supplement, these specific 
requirements are in Part 4 - Agency Program Requirements or Part 5 - Clusters of Programs, as 
applicable. This type of compliance requirement specifies the activities that can or cannot be 
funded under a specific program. 

Audit Objectives 

Determine whether Federal awards were expended only for allowable activities. 

Suggested Audit Procedures 

1 . Identify the types of activities which are either specifically allowed or prohibited by the 
laws, regulations, and the provisions of contract or grant agreements peitaining to the 
program. 

2. When allowability is determined based upon summary level data, perform procedures to 
verify that; 

a. Activities were allowable. 

b. Individual ti-ansactions were properly classified and accumulated into the activity 
total. 

3. When allowability is determined based upon individual transactions, select a sample of 
transactions and perform procedures to verify fliat the transaction was for an allowable 
activity. 

4. The auditor should be alert for large transfers of funds fi’om program accounts which may 
have been used to fund unallowable activities.

B. ALLOWABLE COSTS/COST PRINCIPLES 

Applicability of OMB Cost Principles Circulars 

The following OMB cost principles circulars prescribe the cost accounting policies associated 
with the administration of Federal awards by non-profit organizations, States, local governments, 
and Indian tribal governments. However, for block gi’ants authorized by the Omnibus Budget 
Reconciliation Act of 1981 and the Job Training Partnership Act, State rules for expenditures of 
State funds apply (Appendix 1). Federal awards include Federal programs and cost-type 
conti-acts and may be in the fonn of grants, contracts, and other agreements. 

OMB Circular A-87, "Cost Principles for State, Local and Indian Tribal Governments" 

OMB Circular A-2 1 , "Cost Principles for Educational Institutions" 

OMB Circular A-122, "Cost Principles for Non-Profit Organizations" 

States, local governments, and Indian tribal governments ai’e subject to OMB Circular A-87. All 
institutions of higher education are subject to the cost principles contained in OMB Circular 
A-21 which incoiporates the four Cost Accounting Standai’ds Boai’d (CASB) Standai'ds and the 
Disclosure Statement (DS-2) requirements as described in paragi aphs C. 10 through C.14 and 
Appendices A and B of OMB Circular A-21 . Non-profit organizations are subject to OMB 
Circulai’ A-122, except those non-profit organizations listed in Attachment C of OMB Circulai’ 
A-122. These non-profit organizations ai'e not subject to OMB.Circular A-122 but are subject to 
the commercial cost principles contained in'the Federal Acquisition Regulation (FAR). Also, by 
confract teims and conditions, some non-profit organizations may be subject to the CASB's 
Standai'ds and the Disclosure Statement (DS-1) requirements. 

Federal awards administered by publicly-owned hospitals and other providers of medical care are 
exempt fi'om OMB's cost principles circulars, but ai'e subject to requirements promulgated by the 
sponsoring Federal agencies (45 CFR part 74, appendix E). 

The cost principles applicable to anon-Federal entity apply to all Federal awai'ds received by the 
entity, regardless of whether the awards are received directly fi'om the Federal Government or 
indirectly through a pass-through entity. 

The circulars describe selected cost items, allowable and unallowable costs, and standard 
methodologies for calculating indirect costs rates (e.g., methodologies used to recover facilities 
and administi'ative costs (F&amp;A) at institutions of higher education). 

The cost principles ailiculated in the three circulars are in most cases substantially identical but a 
few differences do exist. These differences are necessaiy because of the nature of the 
Federal/State/local/non-profit organization relationship, programs administered, and breadth of 
seiwices offered by some grantees and not others. Exhibit 1 , Selected Cost Items Not Treated the 
Same Among the Circulai's, lists selected cost items for which treatment are not substantially 



O 



133 Compliance Supplemenl 



3-B-l 



March 2000 



Compliance Requirements 



identical among the cost principles circulai-s. Exhibit 2, Selected Unallowable Cost Items, lists 
selected items that are unallowable in one or more of the cost principles circulars. 

Compliance Requirements - Allowability of Costs - General Criteria (applicable to both 
direct and indirect costs) 

The general criteria affecting allowability of costs under Federal awards are: 

Costs must be reasonable and necessary for the perfoimance and administration of 
Federal awards. 

Costs must be allocable to the Federal awards under the provisions of the cost 
principles or CASE Standards, as applicable. A cost is allocable to a particular’" 
cost objective (e.g., a specific function, program, project, department, or the like) 
if the goods or services involved ai’e chai’ged or assigned to such cost objective in 
accordance with relative benefits received. 

Costs must be given consistent treatment through application of those generally 
accepted accounting principles appropriate to the circumstances. A cost may not 
be assigned to a Federal award as a direct cost if any other cost incuired for the 
same puipose in like circumstances was allocated to the Federal award as mi 
indirect cost. 

Costs must conform to any limitations or exclusions set forth in the circulars. 

Federal laws, State or local laws, sponsored agreements or other governing 
regulations as to types or amounts of cost items. 

Costs must be net of all applicable credits that result fi-om transactions that reduce 
or offset direct or indirect costs. Examples of such transactions include purchase 
discounts, rebates or allowances, recoveries or indemnities on losses, insurance 
refunds or rebates, and adjustments for overpayments or eiToneous charges. 

Costs must be documented in accordance with 0MB Circular A- 110 for non- 
profit organizations or the A-102 Common Rule for State, local and Indian Tribal 
.governmental units. 

Compliance Requirements - Indirect Costs 

Indirect costs ai'e those costs that benefit common activities and, therefore, cannot be readily 
assigned to a specific direct cost objective or project. 

In order to recover indirect costs, organizations must prepare cost allocation plans (CAPs) which 
apply only to States, local and Indian tribal governments or indirect cost rate proposals (IDCRPs) 
in accordance with the guidelines provided in OMB's circulars. States, major local governments, 
Indian tiibal governments, institutions of higher education, and non-profit organizations must, 
submit CAPs or IDCRPs to the Federal cognizant agency for indirect cost negotiation for 

O A-133 Compliance Supplement 3-B-2 

ERJC 



32 



March 2000 



Compliance Requirements 



approval. Other organizations, such as smaller local governments, must prepai-e the appropriate 
CAPs or IDCRPs and maintain them oh file for review. These other organizations may use the 
allocation methods and indirect cost rate maintained on file for cost recoveiy. 

At institutions of higher education, indirect costs include the following categories: building and 
equipment depreciation or use allowance, operation and maintenance expenses, interest expenses, 
general administrative expenses, departmental administration expenses, libraiy expenses, and 
student administration expenses. 

At non-profit organizations, indirect costs generally include general adminishative costs (e.g., 
the president’s office, payroll, general accounting) and facility costs (e.g., rental costs, operations 
and maintenance, interest expense) that ai'e not heated as direct costs. 

The indirect cost proposals prepai'ed by institutions of higher education and other non-profit 
organizations ai'e based on the most cuiTent financial data supported by the organization’s 
accounting system and audited financial statements. These indirect cost proposals can be used to 
either establish predetemiined or fixed indirect cost rates, or to establish or finalize provisional 
rates. 

There are three types of plans/proposals submitted by States, local governments, and Indian tribal 
governments: 

1 . State a?u.l Local Govemmentwide CAPs'- These plans ai'e used to allocate service 
center costs (or Section I costs) to individual departments and agencies and 
describe the methods used for charging billed costs (or Section II costs) to 
individual user organizations or activities. 

2. Department or Local IDCRP - These rate proposals combine the billed and 
allocated costs fi'om the State-wide or local-wide plan with depailmental or local 
level indirect costs and compute an indirect cost rate to be used in charging 
indirect costs to individual programs and activities. 

3. Public Assistance CAPs - These CAPs describe the methods for allocating 
State-wide or local-wide allocated and/or billed indirect costs and depailmental 
indirect, adminishative, and operating costs of State or local welfare or human 
seiTices organizations to the Medicaid, Food Stamps and welfai'e programs, etc. 
These plans ai'e required by the teims of 45 CFR pail 95, which incoiporates 
0MB Circulai' A-87 by reference, and they must be revised and resubmitted to the 
Federal Government whenever an organizational or programmatic change 
invalidates the cuirently-approved allocation method. 

At States, local governments, and Indian tribal governments, indirect costs are accumulated at 
two levels: the State/local-wide level and the depaitment/agency level. At the State/local-wide 
level, indirect costs include: (1) centi'al service costs that ai'e allocated (refeired to as Section I 
costs, which typically include general accounting, personnel, and purchasing); and, (2) central 
semce costs that ai'e billed (refeired to as Section II costs, which typically include computer 



ERLC -133 Compliance SupplemeiU 



3-B-3 



33 



Mtu*ch 2000 



Compliapue Requii-ements 



sei*vices, motor pool, insurance, and fringe benefits). Certain costs, such as facilities and 
operations and maintenance, can be classified as Section I or Section II costs by State/local 
governments. 

At State or local governmental departments or agencies, where Federal awards are usually canied 
out, indirect costs noimally include the facilities and administrative costs of each department or 
agency and the allocated centi'al service costs distinbuted through the State/local-wide CAP. 
Additionally, Section 11 costs are direct charges to these departments or agencies. As such, these 
direct billings may be charged directly to Federal awards or be included in the depaitment or 
agency indirect cost pools. 

CAPs are comprised of two parts; a nairative section that describe the service cost center and 
allocation methodologies, and a mathematical allocation of these seiwice center costs to the user 
departments using the described allocation methods. CAPs and IDCRPs prepared by States, 
local governments, and Indian tribal governments usually are prepai'ed on a prospective basis 
using'actual financial data for a prior year or budget data for the cuirent yeai'. When the actual 
costs for the year covered by the CAP (or a rate agi'eement with respect to a fixed rate) ai'e 
determined, the difference between the costs recovered based on the CAP (or rate agreement) and 
the costs that would have been recovered had the CAP or rate agreement been based on actual 
results is either canied foiwaid to a subsequent CAP or IDCRP or used to adjust individual 
awai'ds on a retroactive basis, with the approval of the Federal cognizant agency for indirect cost 
negotiation. 

Three different types of indirect cost rates can be used by the Federal cognizant agency for 
indirect cost negotiation: predetennined, fixed, and provisional/final. Predetennined rates are 
established for the cuirent or multiple futurd period(s) based on cuirent data (usually data from 
the most recently ended fiscal year, known as the base period). Predetennined rates are not 
subject to adjustment, except under vei 7 unusual circumstances. Fixed rates are based on cuirent 
data in the same manner as predeteimined rates, except that the difference between the costs of 
the base period used to establish the rate and the actual costs of the cuirent period is canied 
foirvard as an adjustment to the rate computation for a subsequent period. Provisional rates are 
temporaiy rates used for funding and billing indirect costs, pending the establishment of a final 
rate for a period. 

Special Compliance Requirements 

Disclosure Statements 

0MB Circulai' A-21 requires institutions of higher education that receive more than $25 million 
in Federal funding in a fiscal year to prepare and submit a DS-2 that describes the institution’s 
cost accounting practices. These institutions are required to submit a DS-2 within six months 
after the end of the institution's fiscal yeai' that begins after May 8, 1996, unless the institution is 
required to submit a DS-2 eai'lier due to a receipt of a cost accounting standai'd covered contract 
in accordance with 48 CFR section 9903.202-1.

Conpliance Requii*ements 



These institutions ai-e responsible for maintaining an accurate DS-2 and complying with 
disclosed cost accounting practices. They are also responsible for filing amendments to the DS-2 
when disclosed practices ai'e changed or modified. 

Also, by contract teims and conditions, some non-profit organizations may be subject to the 
case's Standai’ds and the DS-1 requirements. 

Large Research Facilities Construction Costs 

0MB Circular A-21 requires that for lai-ge reseai’ch facilities (those with conshiiction costs of 
more than $10 million) of which 40 percent or more of total assignable space is expected for 
Federal use, an educational institution (institution) must maintain an adequate review and 
approval process to ensure that consh-uction costs are reasonable. The review process shall 
addi’ess and document relevant factors affecting consti’uction costs, such as: 

Life cycle costs 

Unique research needs 

Special building needs 

Building site prepai’ation 

Environmental consideration 

Federal constmction code requirements 

Competitive procurement practices 

The approval process shall include review and approval of the projects by the institution's Boai’d 
of Tmstees or other independent entities. 

0MB Circular A-21 also requires that for reseai’ch facilities costing more than $25 million, of 
which 50 percent or more of total assignable space is expected for Federal use, the institution 
must document the review steps peifoimed to assure that construction costs ai-e reasonable. The 
review should include an analysis of constmction costs and a comparison of these costs with 
relevant constmction data, including the National Science Foundation data for research facilities 
based on its biennial sui-vey, "Science and Engineering Facilities at Colleges and Universities." 

Audit Objectives (Both Direct and Indirect Costs) 

Determine whether the organization complied with the provisions of the applicable 0MB cost 
principles circulars (0MB Circulai-s A-87, A-21, A-122) or CASE Standai'^ as follows: 

1 . Direct chai’ges to Federal awai’ds were for allowable costs. 

2. Charges to cost pools used in calculating indirect cost rates were for allowable costs. 

3. For States, local governments, and Indian tribal governments, charges to cost pools 
allocated to Federal awards though CAPs were for allowable costs. 



O ■ 

, Compliance Supplement 



3-B-5 



March 2000 



Compliance Requii-ements 



4. The methods of allocating the costs are in accordance with the applicable cost principles 
or CASE Standards and produce an equitable and consistent distribution of costs (e.g., 
cost allocation bases include all allowable and unallowable base costs to which allowable 
indirect costs are allocable and the cost allocation methodology complies with the 
applicable cost principles and provides equitable and consistent allocation of indirect 
costs to benefitting cost objectives). 

5. Indirect cost rates were applied in accordance with approved rate agi-eements and 
associated billings were the result of applying the approved rate to the proper base 
amount(s). 

6. For States, local governments, and Indian tribal governments, cost allocations were in 
accordance with CAPs approved by the Federal cognizant agency for indirect cost 
negotiation or, in cases where such plans ai'e not subject to approval, in accordance with 
the plan on file. 

7. Cost accounting practice disclosures, described in the DS-1 or DS-2 (including 
amendments), represented actual practice consistently applied. This objective only 
applies to non-Federal entities that are required to submit the DS-1 or DS-2. 

8. The institution's review of large research facilities under construction was documented as 
required. 

Suggested Audit Procedures (Both Direct and Indirect Costs) 

General 

1. The following procedures apply to direct chai'ges to Federal awards as well as to charges 
to cost pools that are allocated wholly or partially to Federal awards, or used in 
foimulating indirect cost rates used for recovering indirect costs from Federal awards. If 
the auditor identifies unallowable costs, the auditor should be aware that "directly 
associated costs" may have been charged. Directly associated costs are costs incuiTed 
solely as a result of incuning another cost, and would not have been incuired if the other 
cost had not been incuired. For example, fringe benefits ai'e "directly associated" with 
payi'oll costs. When an unallowable cost is incuired, directly associated costs ai'e also 
unallowable. 

Test a sample of ti'ansactions for confomance with the following criteria contained in the 
"Basic Guidelines" section of applicable 0MB cost principles circulai's. 

a. For State and local governments, authorized or not prohibited under State or local 
laws or regulations. 

b. Approved by the Federal awai'ding agency, if required. 



O 

ERIC 



A-133 Compliance Supplemenl 



3-B-6 



36 



March 2000 



Conopliapce Requirements 



c. Conform with the allowability of costs provisions of applicable cost principles, or 
limitations in the program agreement, program regulations, or program statute. 

d. Conform with the allocability provisions of applicable cost principles or CASE 
Standai'ds. 

e. Represent chai'ges for actual costs, not budgeted or projected amounts. 

f With respect to fringe benefit allocations, charges, or rates, such allocations, 
chai'ges, or rates ai‘e based on the benefits received by different classes of 
employees within the organization. 

g. Applied unifoimly to Federal and non-Federal activities. 

h. Given consistent accounting ti'eatment within and between accounting periods. 
Consistency in accounting requires that costs incuired for the same purpose, in 
like circumstances, be treated as either direct costs only or indirect costs only with 
respect to final cost objectives. 

i. Calculated in confoimity with CASE Standai'ds, generally accepted accounting 
principles, or another comprehensive basis of accounting, when required under the 
applicable cost principles or CASE Standai'ds. Costs for post-employment 
benefits must be funded to be allowable. 

j. Not included as a cost or usqd to meet cost shai'ing requirements of other 
Federally-supported activities of the cuirent or a prior period. 

k. Net of all applicable credits, e.g., volume or cash discounts, insurance recoveries, 
refunds, rebates, trade-ins, adjustments for checks not cashed, and scrap sales. 

l. Not included as both a direct billing and as a component of indirect costs, e.g., 
excluded fi'om cost pools included in CAPs and/or EDCRPs, if charged directly to 
Federal awards. 

m. Supported by appropriate documentation, such as approved purchase orders, 
receiving reports, vendor invoices, canceled checks, and time and attendance 
records, and coirectly charged as to account, amount, and period. Documentation 
requirements for salaries and wages, and time and effort distribution are described 
in applicable cost principles. Documentation may be in an electronic fom. 

Internal service, central sendee, pension, or similar activities or funds 

2. When material charges ai'e made fi'om internal sei*vice, central sei*vice, pension, or similai' 
activities or funds, the auditor should verify that the charges fi'om these activities or funds 
are in accordance with the applicable cost principles. The auditor should consider 
procedures, such as: 



433 Compliance Supplement 



3-B-7 



March 2000 Compliance Requirements 

a. For activities accounted for in sepai-ate funds, ascertain if: (1) retained 
eamings/fund balances (including reserves) were computed in accordance with the 
applicable cost principles; (2) working capital was not excessive in amount 
(generally not greater than 60 days for cash expenses for noraial operations 
incuired for the period exclusive of depreciation, capital costs and debt principal 
costs); and, (3) refunds were made to the Federal Government for its share of any 
amounts transfeiTed or boiTowed fi'om internal seiv'ice or central service funds for 
purposes other than to meet the operating liabilities, including interest on debt, of 
the fund 

b. Test that all users of seiwices ai'e billed in a consistent manner. 

c. Test that billing rates exclude unallowable costs, in accordance with applicable 
cost principles. 

d. . Test, where activities are not accounted for in separate funds, that billing rates (or 

charges) ai'e developed based on actual costs and were adjusted to eliminate 
profits. 

e. For organizations that have self-insurance and a certain type of fringe benefit 
programs (e.g. pension funds), ascertain if independent actuarial studies 
appropriate for such activities are perforaied at least biennially and that cuirent 
period costs were allocated based on an appropriate study which is not over two 
years old. 

IDCRP (Testing of the proposal) 

3. The IDCRP is based upon costs chai-ged to cost pools representing costs of a base yeai\ 
The base year often precedes the year in which the IDCRP is prepai-ed and the yeai' the 
resulting Indirect Cost Rate Agi-eement (IDCRA) is used to charge indirect costs. For 
example, a non-Federal entity may submit an IDCRP in Januaiy 1998, based upon costs 
incuiTed and charged to cost pools during fiscal yeai- ending June 30, 1997 (1997), the 
base year. The resulting IDCRA negotiated during the year ending June 30, 1 998 (1998) 
would be used as the basis for chai'ging indirect costs to Federal awai’ds in the year ended 
June 30, 1999 (1999). For this example, the tenn IDCRA will also include an IDCRP 
which is not required to be submitted to the Federal agency for indirect cost negotiation 
but is retained on file and is used to charge indirect costs to Federal awai’ds the same as 
an approved plan resulting in an IDCRA. 

An audit timing consideration is that the audit for 1997 (which covers the applicable cost 
pools) may be completed before the IDCRP is submitted. Therefore, as part of the 1997 
audit, the auditor cannot complete testing of the IDCRP! Also, if the auditor waits to test 
the IDCRP until 1999 (the year when this IDCRP is first used to chai’ge Federal awai’ds), 
the auditor would be testing 1997 records, which would then be two yeai’S old. 



" A-133 Compliance Supplement 



3-B-8 



March 2000 



Compliance Requirements 



Continuing this example, when the IDCRA is the basis for material chai-ges to a major 
program in 1999, the auditor for 1999 is required to obtain appropriate assurance that the 
costs collected in the cost pools and allocation methods are in compliance with the 
applicable cost principles. The following are some acceptable options the auditor may 
use to obtain this assurance: 

Perform interim testing of the costs charged to cost pools (e.g., detennine from 
management the cost pools that management expects to include in the IDCRP and 
test the costs charged to those pools for compliance with the cost principles 
circulars) during the 1997 audit. As pai1 of the 1 998 audit, complete testing and 
verify management's representation against the IDCRP finally submitted in 1 998. 

Test costs charged to the cost pools underlying the IDCRP during the audit^pf 
1998, the year immediately following the base yeai-. This would require testing of 
1 997 transactions. 

Wait until 1 999, the year in which chaiges from the IDCRA are material to a 
major progi'am and test costs charged to cost pools (1997) used to prepai-e the 
IDCRP. This is a much more difficult approach because it requires going back 
two yeai’s to audit the cost charged to cost pools of the base year. 

Advantages of the first two methods ai‘e that the testing of the costs charged to the 
cost pools occurs closer to the time when* the transactions occur (which makes the 
testing easier to perform) and should there be audit exceptions, coirective action 
may be taken earlier to minimize questioned costs (which makes audit exceptions 
easier to resolve). When material indirect costs ai‘e charged to any Type A 
progi-am (deteimined in accordance with Circular A-133), auditors ai-e sUongly 
encouraged to use one of the first two methods. This is because under the risk- 
based approach, described in 0MB Circulai’ A- 1 33, all Type A programs ai’e 
required to be considered major programs at least once in eveiy three years and 
the EDCRA is usually used to charge Federal awai'ds for at least three yeais. 

When the auditee submits the IDCRP, the auditee provides written assurances to the 
Federal government that the plan includes only allowable costs. Accordingly, any 
material unallowable costs reflected in the IDCRP should be reported as an audit finding 
in the year in which they are first found by audit. 

An IDCRP may result in an EDCRA that covers only one year, but most often results in a 
multi-year IDCRA. When an IDCRP has been tested in a prior yeai' and this testing 
provides the auditor appropriate audit assurance, in subsequent yeai’s the auditor is only 
required to perfonri tests to ascertain if there have been material changes to the cost 
accounting practices, including practices that would affect either the cost pools or the 
allocation base and, if so, that the Federal cognizant agency for indirect cost negotiation 
has been informed. 



)3 Compliance Supplement 



3-B-9 



March 2000 



Compliance Requirements 

The auditor should take appropriate steps to coordinate testing of costs charged to cost 
pools supporting an IDCRP with the auditee and, as appropriate, with the Federal 
cognizant agency for indirect cost negotiation. The auditor should consider consulting 
with the auditee in the base year and the year in which the IDCRP is submitted to 
detennine the best (e.g., most efficient) alternative under the circumstances. 

The following procedures are applicable when material chai'ges ai'e made to a rriajor 
program based upon an IDCRP: 

a. Ascertain if the IDCRP has been tested in a prior year. 

(1) When the testing perfoiTned in a prior yeai’ provides appropriate atidit 
assurance, fui-ther review of the IDCRP is not required unless there have 
been material changes to cost accounting practices supporting the IDCRP. 
To ascertain if there have been material changes, the auditor should 
inquire of auditee management as to whether any changes have been made 
to the cost accounting practices and the likely effect of these changes. 

(2) When the auditor believes the changes in cost accounting practices ai-e 
material, and the auditee is required the file the IDCRP with a Federal 
cognizant agency for indirect cost negotiation, the auditor should ascertain 
if the Federal cognizant agency‘for indirect cost negotiation has been 
appropriately notified of the changes in cost accounting practices. For 
non-Federal entities that are required to file a DS-1 or DS-2, this testing is 
perfoimed in Step 6 "DS-1 and DS-2 Requirements." 

When prior testing of the IDCRP does not provide appropriate audit assurance (e.g, was 
not performed). 

b. Test the cost pools which fonri the basis of the IDCRP and the resulting chai'ges 
to Federal awards to ascertain if they include only allowable costs in accordance 
with the cost principles or CASE Standards, as applicable. Suggested audit • • 
procedure number 1 provides guidance for specific tests. 

G« Test the methods of allocating the costs to ascertain if they ai'e in accordance with 
the provisions of the cost principles or CASE Standards, as applicable, and 
produce an equitable distribution of costs. Appropriate detailed tests may include: 

(1) Test statistical data (e.g., squai'e footage, case counts, salai’ies and wages) 
to ascertain if the proposed allocation or rate bases are reasonable, updated 
as necessaiy, and do not contain any material omissions. 

(2) Review time studies or time and effort reports (where and if used) to 
ascertain if they ai'e mathematically, and statistically accurate, are 
implemented as approved, and ai'e based on the actual effort devoted to the 



A- 133 Compliance Supplement 



3-B-lO 



March 2000 



Compliance Requirements 

vaiious functional and programmatic activities to which the salary and 
wage costs are chai'ged. 

(3) Review the allocation methodology for consistency and test the 
appropnateness of methods used to make changes. 

CAP (Testing of the plan) 

4. Since costs allocated through CAPs may include current year and prior year* costs, the 
auditor should test the costs charged to cost pools suppoi1;ing CAPs and the methods of 
allocating costs from CAPs in each year* when these costs ai'e material to a major 
progr'am. The auditor should consider the following procedures: 

a. Test the cost pools which form the basis of the CAP and the resulting charges to 
Federal awar'ds to ascertain if they include only allowable costs in accordance 
with the applicable cost principles. Suggested audit procedure number I provides 
guidance for specific tests. 

b. Test the methods of allocating the costs to ascertain if they are in accordance with 
the applicable provisions of the cost principles and produce an equitable 
distribution of costs. Appropriate detailed tests may include: 

(1) Test statistical data (e.g., squar'e footage, case counts, salaries and wages) 
to ascertain if the proposed allocation or rate bases are reasonable, updated 
as necessary, and do not contain any material omissions. 

(2) Review time studies or time and effort reports (where and if used) to 
ascertain if they ar'e mathematically and statistically accurate, are 
implemented as approved, and are based on the actual effort devoted to the 
various functional and programmatic activities to which the salary and 
wage costs are charged. 

(3) Review the allocation methodology for consistency and test the 
appropriateness of methods use to make charges changes. 

IDCRA and CAPs (Testing of charges based upon plans) 

5. Perform the following procedures to test the application of charges to Federal awar'ds 
based upon an IDCRA and a CAP. 

a. Ascertain if material indirect costs or centralized or administrative ser'vices costs 
were allocated or charged to a major program. If not, the following suggested 
audit procedures b thr'ough e do not apply. 

b. Obtain and r'ead the current IDCRA and/or CAP and determine the terms in effect.

Indirect Cost Rate Agreements 

c. Select a sample of claims for reimbursement and verify that the rates used are in 
accordance with the rate agreement, that rates were applied to the appropriate 
bases, and that the amounts claimed were the product of applying the rate to the 
applicable base. Verify that the costs included in the base(s) are consistent with 
the costs that were included in the base yeai’ (e.g., if the allocation base is total 
direct costs, verify that cument year direct costs do not include costs items that 
were treated as indirect costs in the base yeai’). 

Public Assistance CAPs (for States, local governments, and Indian tribal governments 

only) 

d. Verify that the methods of charging costs to Federal awai’ds are in accordance 
with the provisions of the approved CAP or prepared CAP on file. 

State and Local Government-Wide CAPs 

e. Ascertain if the amounts used for reimbursement of central service costs for 
Federal awards were in accordance with the approved CAPs or plans on file, when 
approval is not required. 

DS-I and DS-2 Requirements (For applicable non-Federal entities) 

6. Perform the following procedures for the DS-1 or DS-2, as applicable: 

a. Read the DS-1 or DS-2 and its amendments and ascertain if the disclosures agree 
with the policies prescribed in the institution's policies and procedures documents. 

b. Test that the disclosures agi’ee with actual practices for the period covered by 
audit, including whether the practices were consistent throughout the period. 

Large Research Facilities Construction Costs (For applicable educational institutions) 

7. Perform the following procedures related to large research facilities: 

a. Ascertain if the institution had large research facilities as defined in 0MB 
Circula]’ A-2 1 under constmction. If not the following suggested audit procedures 
b and c do not apply. 

b. For large research facilities under construction of which 40 percent or more of 
total assignable space is expected for Federal use, review the institution's approval 
process that should include boai'd minutes or other documentation to ascertain if 
the institution's Board of Trustees or other independent entity reviewed and 
approved these construction projects.

50 percent or more of total assignable space is expected for Federal use, ascertain

construction costs are reasonable.

Compliance Requirements 



C. CASH MANAGEMENT 



Compliance Requirements 

When entities are funded on a reimbursement basis, program costs must be paid for by entity 
funds before reimbursement is requested from the Federal Government. When funds are 
advanced, recipients must follow procedures to minimize the time elapsing between the transfer 
of funds from the U.S. Treasuiy and disbursement. When advance payment procedures are used, 
recipients must establish similar procedures for subrecipients. 

Pass-through entities must establish reasonable procedures to ensure receipt of reports on 
subrecipients' cash balances and cash disbursements in sufficient time to enable the pass-through 
entities to submit complete and accurate cash t-ansactions reports to the Federal awarding agency 
or pass-through entity. Pass-through entities must monitor cash di'awdowns by their 
subrecipients to assure that subrecipients confoim substantially to the same standai'ds of timing 
and amount as apply to the pass-through entity. 

Interest earned on advances by local government gi-antees and subgrantees is required to be 
submitted promptly, but at least quarterly, to the Federal agency. Up to $100 per yeai- may be 
kept for administrative expenses. Interest earned by non-State nonprofit entities on Federal fund 
balances in excess of $250 is required to be remitted to Department of Health and Human 
Services, Payment Management System, P.O. Box 6021, Rockville, MD 20852. 

Treasuiy regulations at 3 1 CFR part 205, which implement the Cash Management Improvement 
Act of 1990 (CMIA) (Pub. L. No. 101-453), require State recipients to enter into agreements 
which prescribe specific methods of di'awing down Federal funds (funding techniques) for 
selected lai-ge programs. The agreements also specify the teims and conditions in which an 
interest liability would be incuired. Progi'ams not covered by a Treasuiy-State Agreement ai-e 
subject to procedui'es prescribed by Treasury in Subpail B of 3 1 CFR part 205 (Subpail B). 

The requirements for cash management ai'e contained in the 0MB Circulai* 102 (Pai'agraph 2. a.), 

the A-102 Common Rule (§ .21), 0MB Circular A- 11 O' (§ .22), Treasury regulations.at 

3 1 CFR pait 205, Federal awarding agency regulations, and the teims and conditions of the 
awai'd. 

Availability of Other Information 

The U.S. Treasuiy, Financial Management Service maintains a Cash Management Improvement 
Act page on the Internet (http://www.fms.treas.gov/cmia/). 

Audit Objectives 
Deteimine whether: 

1 . The recipient/subrecipient followed procedures to minimize the time elapsing between 
the transfer of funds from the U.S. Treasuiy, or pass-through entity, and their 
disbui-sement. 



O 



“-133 Compliance Supplemen! 



3-C-l 



Mai'ch 2000 



Compliance Requii*emeuts 



2. States have complied with the ternis and conditions of the Treasuiy-State Agreement or 
Subpart B procedures prescribed by Treasury. 

3. The pass-through entity implemented procedures to assure that subrecipients confoimed 
substantially to the same timing requirements that apply to the pass-through entity. 

4. Interest earned on advances was reported/remitted as required. 

Suggested Audit Procedures 

Note: The following procedures ai-e intended to be applied to each program detemiined to be 

major. However, due to the nature of cash management and the system of cash management in 

place in a paiticulai' entity, it may be appropriate and more efficient to perfoim these procedures 

for all programs collectively rather than sepai'ately for each program. 

States 

1 . For programs tested as major for States, verify which of those programs ai'e covered by 
the Treasuiy-State Agreement in accordance with the materiality thresholds in Appendix 
A to subpart A of 3 1 CFR part 205 (3 1 CFR section 205.4). 

2. For those programs identified in procedure 1 , detemiine the funding techniques used for 
those programs. For those funding techniques that require cleai'ance patterns to schedule 
the transfer of funds to the State, review documentation supporting the clearance pattern 
and verify that the clearance pattern confoims to the requirements for developing arid 
maintaining clearance patterns as specified in the Treasui 7 -State Agreement (3 1 CFR 
sections 205.8 and 205.9(b)(4)). 

3. - Select a sample of Federal cash draws and verify that: 

a. The timing of the Federal cash draws were in compliance with the applicable 
funding techniques specified in the Treasuiy-State Agreement or Subpart B 
procedures, whichever is applicable (31 CFR sections 205.7 and 205.20). 

c.« To the extent available, program income, rebates, refunds, and other income and 
leceipts were disbursed before requesting additional Federal cash draws as 
required by the A-102 Common Rule (§_.21) and 0MB Circular A-1 10 
(§_- 22 ). 

4. Where applicable, select a sample of reimbursement requests and trace to supporting 
documentation showing that the costs for which reimbursement was requested were paid 
prior to the date of the reimbursement request (3 1 CFR section 205.7(c)(5)). 

5. Review the calculation of the interest obligation owed to or by the Federal Government, 
reported on the annual report submitted by the State to ascertain that the calculation was 
in accordance with Treasui^ regulations and the tenns of the Treasuiy-State Agreement 

3-C-2 . - ~ .. . .. . 



A- 133 Compliance Supplement 



March 2000 



Compliance Requiremeats 



or Subpart B procedures. Trace amounts used in the calculation to supporting 
documentation. 

States and Other Recipients 

6. For those progi-ams where Federal cash draws are passed through to subrecipients; 

a. Select a representative sample of subrecipients and ascertain the procedures 

implemented to assure that subrecipients minimize the time elapsing between the 
transfer of Federal funds from the recipient and the pay out of funds for progi'am 
puiposes (A- 102 Common Rule§ .37(a)(4)). 

b. Select a representative sample of Federal cash draws by subrecipients anE 
ascertain that they conformed to the procedures. 

Other Recipients and Suhrecipients 

7. For those programs which received advances of Federal funds, ascertain the procedures 
established with the Federal agency or pass-through entity to minimize the time between 
the transfer of Federal funds and the pay out of funds for program purposes.

disbursement were followed 

b. To the extent available, program income, rebates, refunds, and other income and 

receipts were disbursed before requesting additional cash payments as required by 
the A-102 Common Rule (§ .21) and 0MB Circulai' A-1 10 (§ .22). 

9. Where appli cabl e, select a sample of reimbursement requests and trace to supporting, 
documentation showing that the costs for which reimbursement was.requested were paid 
prior to the date of the reimbursement request.

evidence to ascertain whether it was returned to the appropriate agency.

D. DAVIS-BACON ACT 



Compliance Requirements 

When required by the Davis Bacon Act, the Department of Labor's (DOL) govemmentwide 
implementation of the Davis-Bacon Act, or by Federal program legislation, all laborere and 
mechanics employed by conhactors or subcontractors to work on consti’uction confracts in excess 
of $2000 financed by Federal assistance funds must be paid wages not less than those established 
for the locality of the project (prevailing wage rates) by the DOL (40 USC 276a to 276a-7). 

Availability of Other Information 

The U.S. Department of Labor, Employment Standards Adminisfration, maintains a Davis-Bacon 
and Related Acts Internet page (http://www.dol.gov/dol/esa/public/progi-ams/dbra/index.html). 

Audit Objective 

Detei-mine whether the non-Federal entity ensured that contractors and subconti'actois paid 
prevailing wage rates for projects covered by the Davis-Bacon Act. 



Suggested Audit Procedures 

1 . Ascertain if the non-Federal entity receives F.; ieral funds for consti-uction projects; if so, 
review program/project requirements to ascertain if the program/project is covered by the 
Davis-Bacon Act. 

2. Select a sample of consti-uction contracts and subconhacts and verify that the required 
prevailing wage rate clauses were included in conti-acts for consti-uction which exceed 
$ 2000 . 

3 . Deteimine the prevailing wage rates applicable at the time of the construction payi-oll. 
(DOL's Wage and Hour Division publishes a Register of Wage Deteiminations. 
Subscribers to the Davis-Bacon Wage Determination Database on FedWorld’s Website 
can obtain wage determinations online atwww.fedworld.gov.) 

4. Examine a sample of contractor or subcontractor payroll submissions and certifications 
and ascertain if such submissions indicate that laborers and mechanics weie paid the 
prevailing wage rates established by the DOL for the locality at the time of the 
constmction payroll.

E. ELIGIBILITY 

Compliance Requirements 

The specific requirements for eligibility ai’e unique to each Federal program and are found in the 
laws, regulations, and the provisions of contract or grant agi’eements pertaining to the program. 
For progi’ams listed in the Compliance Supplement, these specific requirements are in Pait 4 - 
Agency Program Requirements or Part 5 - Clusters of Programs, as applicable. This compliance 
requirement specifies the criteria for deteimining the individuals, groups of individuals, or 
subrecipients that can participate in the program and the amounts for which they qualify. 

Audit Objectives 

Determine whether: 

1. Required eligibility deteiminations were made, (including obtaining any required 
documentation/verifications) and that individual program participants or groups of 
paiticipants (including area of seiwice delivery) were deteimined to be eligible. Only 
eligible individuals or gi’oups of individuals (including area of service delivery) 
participated in the program. 

2. Subawai’ds were made only to eligible subrecipients. 

3. Amounts provided to or on behalf of eligibles were calculated in accordance with 
program requirements. 

Suggested Audit Procedures 

1 . Eligibility for Individuals 

a. For some Federal progi’ams with a large number of people receiving benefits, the 
non-Federal entity may use a computer system for processing individual 
eligibility deteiminations and delivery of benefits. Often these computer systems 
are complex and will be separate from the non-Federal entity's regular financial 
accounting system. Typical ftmctions a computer system for eligibility may 
perfoim are;

amount of benefits 

Pay benefits (e.g., write checks) 

Maintain eligibility records, including infoimation about each individual 
and benefits paid to or on behalf of the individual (regular payments, 
refunds, and adjustments) 

Track the period of time an individual is eligible and stop benefits at the 
end of a predetermined period unless, there is a redetermination of 
eligibility 

Perfoim matches with other computer data bases to verify eligibility (e.g., 
matches to verify earnings or identify individuals who are deceased)
